Four-point functions and contact terms from higher-spin Ward identities of Chern-Simons-matter theory
Trivko Kukolj

Abstract
Large N N quasifermionic ( Q ​ F QF ) Chern-Simons-matter theories exhibit weakly-broken higher-spin symmetry and contain an infinite-dimensional algebra of almost-conserved higher-spin currents. By analyzing local higher-spin Ward identities, we constrain the higher-spin algebra of this theory, for operators of spin s < 4 s<4 . The solution interpolates smoothly between Ward identities of free-fermionic and critical-bosonic U ⁡ ( N ) U(N) models, in accordance with the bosonization duality. At finite ’t Hooft coupling, we decompose four-point current correlators involving ( ∂ ⋅ J 3 ) Q ​ F (\partial\cdot J_{3})^{QF} in terms of free theory structures and identify possible epsilon-transform relations for Q ​ F QF four-point functions. Additionally, we find that higher correlators of the theory develop scheme-independent higher-spin contact terms, stemming from the presence of a background Chern-Simons term in the effective action. These terms are related to similar contact terms of the associated quasibosonic Chern-Simons-matter theory via Legendre transform. The results are supplemented by perturbative checks, including collinear limit computations of ⟨ T − ⁣ − ​ J + ​ J + ⟩ Q ​ F {\langle}T_{-{}-}J_{+}J_{+}{\rangle}_{QF} , ⟨ T − ⁣ − ​ J + ​ J + ​ O ⟩ Q ​ F {\langle}T_{-{}-}J_{+}J_{+}O{\rangle}_{QF} and ⟨ J − − − J + J + J + ⟩ Q ​ F {\langle}J_{-{}-{}-}J_{+}J_{+}J_{+}{\rangle}_{QF} .



1
Introduction

Three dimensional conformal field theories of matter minimally coupled to Chern-Simons gauge interactions exhibit remarkable properties in several aspects. Generalizing the well-understood rank/level duality of pure Chern-Simons theory
[
1
]
, such models commonly appear in recent literature thanks to an abundance of strong-weak dualities relating bosons and fermions. In its simpler form, this 3d bosonization duality relates non-supersymmetric theories containing fundamental matter with unitary or orthogonal gauge groups
[
2
,
3
]
, although several extensions have been investigated
[
4
,
5
]
, including supersymmetric theories where the correspondence arises from three-dimensional Seiberg dualities
[
6
,
7
]
.

More than just field theory duals, Chern-Simons-matter theories arise as holographic duals of interacting higher-spin gravity theories on
AdS_{4}
. Indeed, the initial reasoning for the study of various Chern-Simons-matter CFTs came from generalizing the duality between type A and type B Vasiliev theories and free/critical bosonic/fermionic vector models
[
8
,
9
]
. These dualities are best understood in the large
N
limit, where both sides are weakly coupled and can be studied perturbatively. On the gravity side, this appears as the classical limit of higher-spin theory, with a bulk coupling constant proportional to
1/\sqrt{N}
. Conversely, at finite
N
, these dualities should provide us with quantum generalizations of gravity, involving a tower of massless higher-spin particles. Recent works have pointed out the existence of UV complete chiral higher-spin theories, equipped with a local action
[
10
,
11
]
, further motivating the study Chern-Simons-matter theories as toy models of
AdS_{4}/CFT_{3}
.

Higher-spin gauge invariance in the bulk manifests as an infinite-dimensional higher-spin symmetry of the dual CFT. This was shown to be rather constraining, as the only theories with exact higher-spin symmetry are essentially free theories
[
12
]
. By introducing Chern-Simons interactions, one breaks the symmetry at subleading orders in
N
. The previously conserved higher-spin currents
J_{s}
now develop divergences in the form of multi-trace operators:

\displaystyle(\partial\cdot J_{s})\propto\frac{1}{N}[J_{s_{1}}][J_{s_{2}}]+\frac{1}{N^{2}}[J_{s_{1}}][J_{s_{2}}][J_{s_{3}}]+\mathcal{O}\bigg(\frac{1}{N^{3}}\bigg),\quad\text{ for }s>2

(1.1)

Nevertheless, for a specific class of large
N
theories, slightly broken higher-spin symmetry can still be leveraged to constrain correlation functions
[
13
]
. Furthermore, in the usual sense, it allows one to build conserved charges and discuss variations of operators, to leading order in large
N
, giving rise to an algebra of higher-spin currents. The main goal of this work is to investigate the application of these techniques to the theory of Dirac fermions gauged by
U(N)_{k}
Chern-Simons interactions in the large
N
, ’t Hooft limit
N\to\infty,k\to\infty
with
N/k
fixed. This theory is often referred to as the quasifermionic (QF) Chern-Simons-matter theory
[
14
,
15
]
, and is conjectured to be dual
1
1

1

Outside the large
N
, ’t Hooft limit, the duality is slightly more subtle, as argued in
[
3
,
16
]
.
to the theory of Wilson-Fisher scalars coupled to a
U(k)_{-{\text{sign}}(k)N}
Chern-Simons gauge field. We analyze three- and four-point functions by writing local higher-spin Ward identities of the spin-3 current. At separated points, this leads to a decomposition of QF correlators into a small number of well known kinematic structures.

Of special interest is the analysis of higher-spin contact terms. QF correlators of spinning currents may develop contributions at coincident points, stemming from the presence of background field Chern-Simons terms in the effective action. As argued in
[
17
]
, the fractional parts of these terms correspond to intrinsic observables of the theory. They already arise when discussing the two-point function of the vector current or the stress tensor, which develop parity-breaking contributions consistent with current conservation. Imposing higher-spin symmetry locally leads to a systematic set of constraints, from which one can answer how higher correlators of the theory are affected by such scheme-independent contact terms. This serves to further probe the dualities between the bosonic and fermionic descriptions at large
N
, as well as to better understand the higher-spin gravity dual.

The remainder of Section
1
lists the conventions used throughout the text. Section
2
introduces the basics of general higher-spin algebras and associated Ward identities, as well as their computation in free theories. Section
3
details local Ward identities of the critical boson theory, as the simplest example of an interacting CFT with weakly broken higher-spin symmetry. Section
4
presents the constraints on the quasifermionic algebra, including results for four-point functions and higher-spin contact terms. Section
5
contains collinear limit computations of quasifermionic correlators and perturbative checks of spin-3 Ward identities. Section
6
summarizes the main results and discusses possible extensions of this work. Lengthier technical results are collected in appendices.

1.1
Normalizations and Conventions

We will use the following abbreviations to denote operators/correlators of different three-dimensional theories:
FF = free fermionic
U(N)
model; FB = free bosonic
U(N)
model; CF = critical fermionic
U(N)
model; CB = critical bosonic
U(N)
model; QF = quasifermionic theory; QB = quasibosonic theory. Our conventions for free theory operators are:

Free Boson Theory

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} O^{FB}&=8{\phi^{\dagger}}{\phi}\\
J^{FB}_{\mu}&={\phi^{\dagger}}\bigg(i\overleftarrow{\partial_{\mu}}-i\overrightarrow{\partial_{\mu}}\bigg){\phi}\\
T^{FB}_{\mu\nu}&={\phi^{\dagger}}\bigg(-\frac{1}{4}\overleftarrow{\partial_{\mu}}\overleftarrow{\partial_{\nu}}-\frac{1}{4}\overrightarrow{\partial_{\mu}}\overrightarrow{\partial_{\nu}}+\frac{3}{2}\overleftarrow{\partial_{(\mu}}\overrightarrow{\partial_{\nu)}}+\frac{g_{\mu\nu}}{12}\overleftarrow{\partial^{2}}+\frac{g_{\mu\nu}}{12}\overrightarrow{\partial^{2}}-\frac{g_{\mu\nu}}{2}\overleftarrow{\partial_{\sigma}}\overrightarrow{\partial^{\sigma}}\bigg){\phi}\\
J^{FB}_{\mu\nu\rho}&={\phi^{\dagger}}\bigg(\frac{1}{6}\overleftarrow{\partial_{\mu}}\overleftarrow{\partial_{\nu}}\overleftarrow{\partial_{\rho}}-\frac{1}{6}\overrightarrow{\partial_{\mu}}\overrightarrow{\partial_{\nu}}\overrightarrow{\partial_{\rho}}+\frac{5}{2}\overleftarrow{\partial_{(\mu}}\overrightarrow{\partial_{\nu}}\overrightarrow{\partial_{\rho)}}-\frac{5}{2}\overleftarrow{\partial_{(\mu}}\overleftarrow{\partial_{\nu}}\overrightarrow{\partial_{\rho)}}-g_{(\mu\nu}\overleftarrow{\partial^{\sigma}}\overrightarrow{\partial_{\rho)}}\overrightarrow{\partial_{\sigma}}+g_{(\mu\nu}\overleftarrow{\partial_{\rho)}}\overleftarrow{\partial^{\sigma}}\overrightarrow{\partial_{\sigma}}\bigg){\phi}\\
&\quad+{\phi^{\dagger}}\bigg(-\frac{1}{2}g_{(\mu\nu}\overleftarrow{\partial_{\rho)}}\overrightarrow{\partial^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}g_{(\mu\nu}\overrightarrow{\partial_{\rho)}}\overleftarrow{\partial^{2}}-\frac{1}{10}g_{(\mu\nu}\overleftarrow{\partial_{\rho)}}\overleftarrow{\partial^{2}}+\frac{1}{10}g_{(\mu\nu}\overrightarrow{\partial_{\rho)}}\overrightarrow{\partial^{2}}\bigg){\phi}\end{aligned}

(1.2)

Free Fermion Theory

In three dimensions, the generators of the Dirac algebra are given by Pauli matrices
\gamma^{\mu}=\sigma^{\mu}
, for
\mu=1,2,3
. The conjugate Dirac spinor field is now given by
{\bar{\psi}}={\psi}^{\dagger}
. The algebra satisfies:

\displaystyle[{\gamma}^{\mu},{\gamma}^{\nu}]=2i{\epsilon}^{\mu\nu\rho}{\gamma}_{\rho};\qquad\{\gamma^{\mu},\gamma^{\nu}\}=2g^{\mu\nu}\mathds{1}_{2\times 2}\text{ with }g^{\mu\nu}=diag(1,1,1)=\delta^{\mu\nu}

(1.3)

The current operators for the theory of a single massless Dirac fermion are defined as:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} O^{FF}&={\bar{\psi}}{\psi}\\
J^{FF}_{\mu}&=i{\bar{\psi}}{\gamma}_{\mu}{\psi}\\
T^{FF}_{\mu\nu}&={\bar{\psi}}\bigg(-\frac{1}{2}{\gamma}_{(\mu}\overrightarrow{\partial_{\nu)}}+\frac{1}{2}\overleftarrow{\partial_{(\nu}}{\gamma}_{\mu)}+\frac{1}{6}g_{\mu\nu}\overrightarrow{{\not{\partial}}}-\frac{1}{6}g_{\mu\nu}\overleftarrow{\partial^{\sigma}}{\gamma}_{\sigma}\bigg){\psi}\\
J^{FF}_{\mu\nu\rho}&={\bar{\psi}}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\overleftarrow{\partial_{(\mu}}\overleftarrow{\partial_{\nu}}{\gamma}_{\rho)}+\frac{1}{2}{\gamma}_{(\mu}\overrightarrow{\partial_{\nu}}\overrightarrow{\partial_{\rho)}}-\frac{5}{3}\overleftarrow{\partial_{(\mu}}{\gamma}_{\nu}\overrightarrow{\partial_{\rho)}}+\frac{g_{(\mu\nu}}{3}\overleftarrow{\partial^{\sigma}}{\gamma}_{\rho)}\overrightarrow{\partial_{\sigma}}\bigg){\psi}\\
&\quad-{\bar{\psi}}\bigg(\frac{g_{(\mu\nu}}{5}\overrightarrow{\partial_{\rho)}}\overrightarrow{{\not{\partial}}}+\frac{g_{(\mu\nu}}{5}\overleftarrow{\partial_{\rho)}}\overleftarrow{\partial^{\sigma}}{\gamma}_{\sigma}+\frac{g_{(\mu\nu}}{3}\overleftarrow{\partial_{\rho)}}\overrightarrow{{\not{\partial}}}+\frac{g_{(\mu\nu}}{3}\overleftarrow{\partial^{\sigma}}{\gamma}_{\sigma}\overrightarrow{\partial_{\rho)}}-\frac{g_{(\mu\nu}}{10}\overleftarrow{\partial^{2}}{\gamma}_{\rho)}+\frac{g_{(\mu\nu}}{10}{\gamma}_{\rho)}\overrightarrow{\partial^{2}}\bigg){\psi}\end{aligned}

(1.4)

The normalizations are chosen so that two-point functions of the given currents are identical in FF and CB theories. Symmetrization comes with the appropriate
1/n!
factor. In Sections
4
,
5
and appendices, the subscripts
{\langle}...{\rangle}_{FF}
and
{\langle}...{\rangle}_{FB}
will denote one half of the value of those in the theory of a single Dirac fermion and complex boson, respectively, and carry an extra
1/2
factor relative to Sections
1
-
3
. We define all momentum space correlators with
(2\pi)^{3}\delta^{(3)}(\sum p)
factors stripped.

As a shorthand, in the theory labeled by the subscript
X
, we denote the Ward identity where
(\partial\cdot J_{s})
is inserted into the correlation function
{\langle}O_{1}O_{2}...O_{n}{\rangle}_{X}
by
{\langle}[Q_{s},O_{1}O_{2}...O_{n}]{\rangle}_{X}
.

2
Higher-spin algebras

Three-dimensional CFTs possessing at least one conserved higher-spin current have been shown to exactly satisfy higher-spin symmetry, with their correlation functions matching those of free field theories
[
12
]
. Relaxing these requirements, one can next look at large
N
theories where higher-spin symmetry is broken by
1/N
corrections. Two particularly well understood classes of such CFTs were named quasibosonic and quasifermionic theories.

Such theories contain a spectrum of single-trace operators congruent with Vasiliev theories. They have a large
{\tilde{N}}
parameter, which plays a role analogous to
N
in free theories. Depending on the details of the theory
2
2

2

There are examples where the spectrum is truncated, e.g.
O(N)
theories with only even-spin currents
J_{2s}
. In Chern-Simons-matter theories, one can also consider extended operators
[
18
,
19
,
20
]
.
, we generically have an infinite tower of conformal primaries
J_{s}
with integer spin
s>0
and scaling dimension
\Delta_{s}=s+1+\mathcal{O}(1/{\tilde{N}})
. In addition, QB theories contain a scalar
J_{0}
of dimension
\Delta_{0}=1+\mathcal{O}(1/{\tilde{N}})
, while QF theories contain a scalar
\tilde{J_{0}}
of dimension
\Delta_{\tilde{0}}=2+\mathcal{O}(1/{\tilde{N}})
. To leading order the currents are conserved, therefore for each spin-
s
current
J_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}..\mu_{s}}
we can build a (pseudo)charge by integrating its zeroth component over a closed, equal-time surface
\Sigma
:

\displaystyle Q_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}...\mu_{s-1}}=\int_{\Sigma}*J=\int_{\Sigma}J^{0}_{\ \ \mu_{1}\mu_{2}...\mu_{s-1}}

(2.1)

We will mostly consider the full operator
Q_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}...\mu_{s-1}}
, however its specific components can be accessed by first contracting the free indices with a specified conformal Killing tensor
\zeta^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}...\mu_{s-1}}
and then integrating. An example of this in the lightcone frame is given in
[
12
]
. Each charge
Q_{s}
carries spin
S=s-1
and scaling dimension
\Delta=s-1+\mathcal{O}(1/{\tilde{N}})
. Even though QB and QF theories do not have an exact higher-spin symmetry, to leading order, one can write down Ward identities, as derived in
[
13
]
. We emphasize the difference between
local
and
integrated
Ward identities written therein. The latter comes from integrating the former over the insertion of
(\partial\cdot J_{s\geq 3})
, however this difference becomes important when discussing theories with weakly-broken higher-spin symmetry.

As in
[
13
]
, we define the action of higher-spin charges on local operators
[Q_{s},O_{i}]
, which will appear in integrated higher-spin Ward identities. The action of the charge is defined as the finite part of a closed spatial surface integral over the selected operator
3
3

3

Note that here we assume the absence of any other nearby operators.

O_{i}
, with the insertion of the corresponding higher-spin current
J_{s}
. Furthermore,
[Q_{s},O_{i}]
is not sensitive to the choice of this closed surface, so we can imagine a tiny sphere around
O_{i}
whose radius is then taken to zero.

\displaystyle[Q_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}...\mu_{s-1}},O_{i}]=\int_{|x|=r}\hat{n}\cdot J_{s}\times O_{i}|_{\text{finite as }r\to 0}

(2.2)

This variation can be found directly in the case of free theories, as we will show in Sections
2.2
,
2.3
, however one can already draw constraints from using the CFT data, as we will do in Section
2.1
.

2.1
[Q_{s},J_{s^{\prime}}]
from conformal quantum numbers

Assuming a general QF or QB theory, we outline an algorithm for constraining the action of the (pseudo)charge of a slightly conserved higher-spin current
Q_{l+1}=Q_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}..\mu_{l}}
on a general current primary
J_{s}=J_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}...\alpha_{s}}
, up to coefficients, which may be constants or general functions of parameters of the theory. We proceed under the assumption that both the QF scalar current
\tilde{J_{0}}
and the QB scalar current
J_{0}
are present in the spectrum. The end result for the QF theory is then simply obtained by setting
J_{0}=0
and for the QB theory, by setting
\tilde{J_{0}}=0
.

The anomalous dimensions of current primaries receive corrections only at subleading order in large
{\tilde{N}}
, which will not play a role here. The (pseudo)charge
Q_{l+1}
has dimension
\Delta=l
and spin
S=l
, while a spin
s
current
J_{s}
has dimension
\Delta=s+1
and spin
S=s
. The QF scalar
\tilde{J_{0}}
has dimension
\Delta=2
, but spin
S=0
, so we will treat the case
[Q_{s},\tilde{J_{0}}]
separately of the other generic cases. A general
[Q_{l+1},J_{s}]
has total scaling dimension
\Delta=l+s+1
and total spin
S=l+s
. Schematically, we can write

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} =&A^{1}_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}...\nu_{s+l}\rho_{1}...\rho_{s+l}}\sum_{k=\max\{s-l,0\}}^{s+l}\partial^{\rho_{1}}\partial^{\rho_{2}}...\partial^{\rho_{k}}(J_{s+l-k})^{\rho_{k+1}...\rho_{s+l}}\\
&+B^{1}_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}...\nu_{s+l}\rho_{1}...\rho_{s+l-1}}\partial^{\rho_{1}}\partial^{\rho_{2}}...\partial^{\rho_{s+l-1}}\tilde{J_{0}}\\
&+\frac{1}{{\tilde{N}}}A^{2}_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}...\nu_{s+l}\rho_{1}...\rho_{s+l-1}}\mathcal{O}_{2}^{\rho_{1}...\rho_{s+l-1}}([J_{s_{1}}][J_{s_{2}}])\\
&+\frac{1}{{\tilde{N}}}B^{2}_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}...\nu_{s+l}\rho_{1}...\rho_{s+l-2}}\mathcal{O}_{2}^{\rho_{1}...\rho_{s+l-2}}([J_{s_{1}}][\tilde{J_{0}}])\\
&+\frac{1}{N}C^{2}_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}...\nu_{s+l}\rho_{1}...\rho_{s+l-3}}\mathcal{O}_{2}^{\rho_{1}...\rho_{s+l-3}}([\tilde{J_{0}}][\tilde{J_{0}}])\par+\frac{1}{{\tilde{N}}^{2}}(\text{Triple-trace operators }\mathcal{O}_{3})+...\par\end{aligned}

(2.3)

Contributing single-trace operators are explicitly written in the first two lines on the right-hand side. The lower bound on the right-hand side sum comes from arguments presented in
[
12
]
, while the upper bound comes from exhausting all single trace primaries. By
\mathcal{O}_{k}([A_{1}][A_{2}]...[A_{k}])
we denote
k
-trace operators formed by multiplying single trace primaries
A_{i}
, with derivatives
\partial^{\rho_{j}}
sprinkled in. For fixed
s
and
l
, there is a finite number of such terms. For each
k
we form all possible
\mathcal{O}_{k}
by matching spin and scaling to the total spin and scaling dimension of the left-hand side. After listing all double-trace contributions
\mathcal{O}_{2}
, we list all triple-trace contributions
\mathcal{O}_{3}
, and so forth, until we exhaust all possible
\mathcal{O}_{n}
.

By construction, all
\rho
’s are dummy indices, which we seek to replace by
\{\nu_{1},\nu_{2},...,\nu_{s+l}\}=\{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}...,\mu_{l}),

(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},...\alpha_{s})\}
4
4

4

The
()
brackets here denote symmetrizing over
\alpha
’s and
\rho
’s separately.
.
A^{1}
,
B^{1}
,
A^{2}
,
B^{2}
,
C^{2}
, and so forth represent general Lorentz invariant structures that we can use to make these replacements, each giving us a specific term on the right-hand side. These are built out of the metric and Levi-Civita symbols. Every such structure can be represented by a set of numbers
(a,b,c,d,e,f,g)
with each letter corresponding to the number of appearances of a term from Table
1
.

:

For each
A^{1}
,
B^{1}
,
A^{2}
, and so on, finding all possible terms in
[Q_{l+1},J_{s}]
now reduces to finding all possible sets of integers
a-g
satisfying certain constraints. Let’s focus on
A^{1}
, with the other terms following analogously.
A^{1}
has
(s+l)

\nu
indices and
(s+l)

\rho
indices. These have to be split between
a-g
terms, therefore we have two equations:

\displaystyle c+d+2e+2f+3g=s+l;\qquad 2a+3b+c+2d+f=s+l

(2.4)

Additionally, we have constraints on each of the integers:

•

a\geq 0
,
c\geq 0
,
e\geq 0
with no further constraints on these integers.

•

Out of three
\nu
indices, two have to be either
\alpha
’s or
\mu
’s. However since
[Q_{l+1},J_{s}]
is symmetric with respect to these separately, any term antisymmetric in three
\nu
indices will not appear. Hence we find
b=0
. Note that this also holds generally for all the Lorentz structures.

•

We need at most one epsilon symbol, since the product of two epsilon symbols can always be written as a sum of products of
g_{\nu_{i}\nu_{j}}
,
g_{\nu_{i}\rho_{j}}
and
g_{\rho_{i}\rho_{j}}
. Therefore
d\geq 0
,
f\geq 0
,
g\geq 0
and
d+f+g\leq 1
. These constraints also hold generally.

•

Further constraints can be imposed, but these are term-specific. For example,
A_{1}
multiplies

\partial^{\rho_{1}}\partial^{\rho_{2}}...\partial^{\rho_{k}}(J_{s+l-k})^{\rho_{k+1}...\rho_{s+l}}
. Therefore multiplying it by any term that is antisymmetric in three
\rho
indices will yield zero. In this case we can say that
g=0
.

We define the twist of each operator to be in the lightcone-minus direction as
\tau=\Delta-s
, where
\Delta
is the scaling dimension and
s
is the number of minus indices. When all
\nu
indices are
\nu_{i}=-
, the pseudo-charge has twist
\tau=0
, and only operators with
\tau=1
can contribute on the right-hand side. This makes all Lorentz structures multiplying multi-trace terms vanish, as well as
B^{1}
, while, as discussed in
[
12
]
, the structure
A^{1}
can be either
g_{-\rho_{1}}g_{-\rho_{2}}...g_{-\rho_{s+l}}
or zero. Referring to table
1
, this constraint forbids all terms
A^{1}
that satisfy
c+2e+2f+3g=s+l
and
c+f=s+l
, except
(a,b,c,d,e,f,g)=(0,0,s+l,0,0,0,0)
. However one can show that indeed this system of equation has no other solutions. For
B^{1}
,
A^{2}
, etc. the constraint forbids all solutions that satisfy
c+2e+2f+3g\leq 0
and
c+f=s+l
. However, this system also does not have any would-be-forbidden solutions. Overall, we see that twist conservation imposes no further constraints.

[Q_{l+1},\tilde{J_{0}}]
can be constrained similarly, by treating
\tilde{J_{0}}
as a spin-1 current, but with one less Lorentz index. The difference being that the Lorentz structures we described now contain one less
\nu
index, e.g.
A^{1}_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}...\nu_{s+l-1}\rho_{1}...\rho_{s+l}}
and so forth. The rest of the discussion follows analogously.

To each term we assign a separate coefficient. The last step is to use the fact that the currents are traceless, bosonic operators. This implies:

\displaystyle g^{\mu_{i}\mu_{j}}[Q_{\mu_{1}..\mu_{i}..\mu_{j}..\mu_{l}},J_{\alpha_{1}..\alpha_{s}}]=0\text{,}\quad\quad g^{\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}}[Q_{\mu_{1}..\mu_{l}},J_{\alpha_{1}..\alpha_{i}..\alpha_{j}..\alpha_{s}}]=0\text{,}\quad\quad{\epsilon}_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3}}J_{s}^{\sigma_{1}}J_{s}^{\sigma_{2}}=0

(2.5)

Which can make certain terms vanish, or relate coefficients of different terms. In some cases, the analysis may be helped by symmetries of the theory. For example, any terms that are not invariant under
\vec{x}\to-\vec{x}
would be forbidden if the theory is parity invariant. The computation now reduces to finding all solutions to the system of constraints. This is easily done using computation packages such as Mathematica, even for larger values of
l
and
s
.

The action of the spin-3 (pseudo)charge on scalar and vector currents will be of primary interest. To simplify the discussion, we assume the CFT has discrete
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry
5
5

5

In the FF theory, this is just part of the larger charge conjugation symmetry, as can be checked using (
1.3
), (
1.4
) and the charge conjugation matrix
C=\gamma^{2}
. This symmetry is still present in the full QF Chern-Simons-matter theory, discussed in Section
4
.
under which
J_{s}\to(-1)^{s}J_{s}
. We write out these commutators for QF and QB theories:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} [Q_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}},\tilde{J_{0}}]_{QF}=&c_{0}^{QF}{\epsilon}_{\rho_{1}\rho_{2}(\mu_{1}}\partial_{\mu_{2})}\partial^{\rho_{1}}J^{\rho_{2}}\\
[Q_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}},J_{\alpha}]_{QF}=&c_{1}^{QF}{\epsilon}_{\alpha\rho(\mu_{1}}\partial_{\mu_{2})}\partial^{\rho}\tilde{J_{0}}+c_{2}^{QF}\partial_{(\mu_{1}}J_{2\mu_{2})\alpha}+c_{3}^{QF}\partial_{\alpha}J_{2\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}+\frac{c_{4}^{QF}}{{\tilde{N}}}{\epsilon}_{\alpha\rho(\mu_{1}}J_{1\mu_{2})}J_{1}^{\rho}\qquad\quad\end{aligned}

(2.6)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} [Q_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}},J_{0}]_{QB}=&c_{0}^{QB}\partial_{(\mu_{1}}J_{\mu_{2})}\\
[Q_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}},J_{\alpha}]_{QB}=&c_{1}^{QB}g_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}\partial_{\alpha}\partial^{2}J_{0}+c_{2}^{QB}g_{\alpha(\mu_{1}}\partial_{\mu_{2})}\partial^{2}J_{0}-(3c_{1}^{QB}+c_{2}^{QB})\partial_{\mu_{1}}\partial_{\mu_{2}}\partial_{\alpha}J_{0}+c_{3}^{QB}\partial_{(\mu_{1}}J_{2\mu_{2})\alpha}\\
&+c_{4}^{QB}\partial_{\alpha}J_{2\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}+\frac{c_{5}^{QB}}{{\tilde{N}}}{\epsilon}_{\rho\alpha(\mu_{1}}J_{0}\partial_{\mu_{2})}\partial^{\rho}J_{0}+\frac{c_{6}^{QB}}{{\tilde{N}}}{\epsilon}_{\rho\alpha(\mu_{1}}\partial^{\rho}J_{0}\partial_{\mu_{2})}J_{0}+\frac{c_{7}^{QB}}{{\tilde{N}}}{\epsilon}_{\alpha\rho(\mu_{1}}J_{1\mu_{2})}J_{1}^{\rho}\end{aligned}

(2.7)

The FB and FF algebras contain the same contributions as those appearing in QB and QF theories, respectively. In the case of FB theories, we denote the algebra coefficients in place of
c_{i}^{QB}
by
b_{i}
, while for FF theories, we replace the
c_{i}^{QF}
coefficients by
a_{i}
. We fix down the constants
a_{i}
and
b_{i}
by direct calculation in Sections
2.2
and
2.3
. One can similarly constrain the algebra for higher spins. For example, the action of the spin-4 (pseudo)charge on the scalar operator of QF theories is given by:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} {}_{QF}=&d_{1}^{QF}{\epsilon}_{\rho_{1}\rho_{2}(\mu_{2}}\partial_{\mu_{2}}\partial^{\rho_{1}}J_{2\mu_{3})}^{\rho_{2}}-2d_{2}^{QF}\partial_{\mu_{1}}\partial_{\mu_{2}}\partial_{\mu_{3}}\tilde{J_{0}}+d_{2}^{QF}g_{(\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}\partial_{\mu_{3})}\partial^{2}\tilde{J_{0}}\\
&-\frac{d_{3}^{QF}}{5{\tilde{N}}}g_{(\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}(J_{1}\cdot\partial)J_{1\mu_{3})}-\frac{d_{3}^{QF}}{5{\tilde{N}}}g_{(\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}J_{1}^{\rho}\partial_{\mu_{3})}J_{1\rho}+\frac{d_{3}^{QF}}{{\tilde{N}}}J_{1(\mu_{1}}\partial_{\mu_{2}}J_{1\mu_{3})}\end{aligned}

(2.8)

And so forth, with the number of possible terms increasing quickly, as we increase
l
and
s
. The analysis also applies to charges built from spin-1 and spin-2 currents. In free theories, with operators given by (
1.2
), (
1.4
), it’s easy to see that all the current operators remain uncharged under the
U(1)
symmetry, while the translation operators built from stress-tensors act as
P_{\nu}^{FB,FF}=\partial_{\nu}
. To simplify notation, we will denote the spin-0,1,2 currents by
O,J_{\mu},T_{\mu\nu}
respectively, in further text.

2.2
Free Fermion algebra from OPE

Free theories are important building blocks for the study of higher-spin symmetry in any number of dimensions. In this context, we will encounter them as limits of Chern-Simons-matter theories. Hence we first study the higher-spin algebra of a three-dimensional theory of a single massless Dirac fermion
{\psi}
. In this case we know the form of all higher-spin currents explicitly
[
21
]
, and the algebra can be computed directly using (
2.2
) and Stokes theorem:

\displaystyle[Q_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}...\mu_{s-1}},O_{i}(0)]=\int_{|x|=r}\hat{n}\cdot J_{s}\times O_{i}|_{\text{finite as }r\to 0}=\int_{V}d^{3}x\ \ \partial_{x}^{\mu}\big(J_{\mu\mu_{1}\mu_{2}...\mu_{s}}(x)\times O_{i}(0)\big)_{\text{finite as }r\to 0}

(2.9)

Which reduces to computing free fermion OPEs. The operator
O_{i}
is placed at the origin, while
V
is the volume of the small sphere encompassing the product, whose radius
r
is taken to zero. Since higher-spin symmetry is exact,
\partial\cdot J_{s}=0
, we only expect the OPE to produce terms diverging as delta functions. Then upon volume integration we only end up with finite terms in the
r\to 0
limit.
We make use of the basic properties of the free fermion propagator:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} S(x)&={\langle}{\bar{\psi}}(x){\psi}(0){\rangle}={\langle}{\psi}(x){\bar{\psi}}(0){\rangle}=-\frac{1}{4\pi}{\not{\partial}}\bigg(\frac{1}{|x|}\bigg);\qquad\qquad{\not{\partial}}S(x)=\partial^{\sigma}S(x){\gamma}_{\sigma}=\delta(x)\end{aligned}

(2.10)

Along with the free fermion equation of motion
{\not{\partial}}{\psi}(x)=\partial^{\sigma}{\bar{\psi}}(x){\gamma}_{\sigma}=0
, as well as properties of the Dirac algebra defined in (
1.3
). We confine ourselves to operators defined in Section
1.1
, however the procedure applies quite generally to currents and charges of any spin. We first analyze the action of the spin-3 charge on the fundamental fields
{\psi}
and
{\bar{\psi}}
. The former reads:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &=\int_{V}\partial^{\mu}\bigg(J_{\mu\nu\rho}(x)\times{\psi}(0)\bigg)\\
&=\int_{V}\partial^{\mu}\bigg[-\frac{1}{2}S(x){\gamma}_{(\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho)}{\psi}(x)-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}S(x){\gamma}_{\rho)}{\psi}(x)+\frac{5}{3}\partial_{\mu}S(x){\gamma}_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}{\psi}(x)\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\par-\frac{1}{3}g_{(\mu\nu}\partial^{\sigma}S(x){\gamma}_{\rho)}\partial_{\sigma}{\psi}(x)+\frac{g_{(\mu\nu}}{5}S(x)\partial_{\rho)}{\not{\partial}}{\psi}(x)+\frac{g_{(\mu\nu}}{5}\partial_{\rho)}\partial^{\sigma}S(x){\gamma}_{\sigma}{\psi}(x)\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad-\frac{g_{(\mu\nu}}{3}\partial_{\rho)}S(x){\not{\partial}}{\psi}(x)-\frac{g_{(\mu\nu}}{3}\partial^{\sigma}S(x){\gamma}_{\sigma}\partial_{\rho)}{\psi}(x)\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\par+\frac{g_{(\mu\nu}}{10}S(x){\gamma}_{\rho)}\partial^{2}{\psi}(x)+\frac{g_{(\mu\nu}}{10}\partial^{2}S(x){\gamma}_{\rho)}{\psi}(x)\bigg]+\cancelto{\to 0\text{ as }r\to 0}{\text{regular}}\end{aligned}

(2.11)

Terms proportional to the equation of motion cancel, and the expression simplifies further after taking the derivative
\partial^{\mu}
. We find:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &=-\frac{1}{3}\int_{V}\delta(x)\bigg(\frac{8}{3}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}{\psi}(x)+\frac{8}{3}{\gamma}_{\alpha}{\gamma}_{(\nu}\partial_{\rho)}\partial^{\alpha}{\psi}(x)\bigg)\end{aligned}

(2.12)

The second term contains a part proportional to the equation of motion for
{\psi}(x)
. By using the properties of (
1.3
), we rewrite it as
{\gamma}_{\alpha}{\gamma}_{(\nu}\partial_{\rho)}\partial^{\alpha}{\psi}(x)=\{{\gamma}_{\alpha},{\gamma}_{(\nu}\}\partial_{\rho)}\partial^{\alpha}{\psi}(x)-{\gamma}_{(\nu}\partial_{\rho)}{\not{\partial}}{\psi}(x)
. Using
\frac{1}{2}\{{\gamma}_{\alpha},{\gamma}_{(\nu}\}=g_{\alpha(\nu}
, we find:

\displaystyle[Q_{\nu\rho},{\psi}]_{FF}

\displaystyle=-\frac{8}{3}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}{\psi}

(2.13)

Following an analogous derivation, we find the action of the spin-3 charge on the Dirac conjugate field
{\bar{\psi}}
:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} [Q_{\nu\rho},{\bar{\psi}}(0)]&=\int_{V}\partial^{\mu}\bigg(J_{\mu\nu\rho}(x)\times{\bar{\psi}}(0)\bigg)=\frac{8}{3}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}{\bar{\psi}}(0)\end{aligned}

(2.14)

We find that the coefficients of (
2.13
) and (
2.14
) differ by a minus sign. Once we’ve fixed down the action of the higher-spin charges on the fundamental fields, we can use this to find the action of the charge on composite operators. Using the definitions of operators (
1.4
), we find commutator of the spin-3 charge with the scalar current
O^{FF}={\bar{\psi}}{\psi}
:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} [Q_{\nu\rho},O]_{FF}&=[Q_{\nu\rho},{\bar{\psi}}]{\psi}+{\bar{\psi}}[Q_{\nu\rho},{\psi}]=\frac{8}{3}{\epsilon}_{\alpha\sigma(\nu}\partial_{\rho)}\partial^{\alpha}J^{\sigma}\end{aligned}

(2.15)

This is consistent with the expression we find in (
2.6
) and furthermore, this is the only possible term allowed by conformal invariance. We now analyze the commutator of the spin-3 charge with the vector current:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} {}_{FF}&=i[Q_{\nu\rho},{\bar{\psi}}]{\gamma}_{\alpha}{\psi}+i{\bar{\psi}}{\gamma}_{\alpha}[Q_{\nu\rho},{\psi}]=\frac{8i}{3}\partial_{(\nu}\bigg(\partial_{\rho)}{\bar{\psi}}{\gamma}_{\alpha}{\psi}-{\bar{\psi}}{\gamma}_{\alpha}\partial_{\rho)}{\psi}\bigg)\end{aligned}

(2.16)

The resulting expression can be written in terms of single-trace primaries, as in (
2.6
). Using the definitions of currents (
1.4
) and disregarding any terms proportional to the equations of motion, we find:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} [Q_{\nu\rho},J_{\alpha}]_{FF}&=-\frac{4}{3}{\epsilon}_{\sigma\alpha(\nu}\partial_{\rho)}\partial^{\sigma}O+\frac{16i}{3}\partial_{(\nu}T_{\rho)\alpha}\end{aligned}

(2.17)

Comparing to (
2.6
), it’s interesting to note that the double-trace spin-1 contribution as well as the
T_{\nu\rho}
term do not contribute in the free fermion theory. However these terms may appear in the QF Chern-Simons-matter theory and we will analyze them in Section
4
.

Equivalently, instead of finding the action of higher-spin charges on currents by using commutation relations of fundamental fields, one may compute the OPE (
2.9
) directly. It is easy to verify that the (
2.15
) and (
2.17
) can be obtained by computing:

\displaystyle[Q_{\nu\rho},O(0)]=\int_{V}\partial^{\mu}\bigg(J_{\mu\nu\rho}(x)\times:{\bar{\psi}}{\psi}:(0)\bigg);\qquad[Q_{\nu\rho},J_{\alpha}(0)]=\int_{V}\partial^{\mu}\bigg(J_{\mu\nu\rho}(x)\times:i{\bar{\psi}}{\gamma}_{\alpha}{\psi}:(0)\bigg)

(2.18)

By the same procedure, we can obtain all other commutators of the free fermion higher-spin algebra.

For comparisons to the remainder of this text, we write the results of this Section as:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &[Q_{\nu\rho},{\psi}]_{FF}=a\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}{\psi};\\
&[Q_{\nu\rho},{\bar{\psi}}]_{FF}=-a\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}{\bar{\psi}};\\
&[Q_{\nu\rho},O]_{FF}=a_{0}{\epsilon}_{\alpha\sigma(\nu}\partial_{\rho)}\partial^{\alpha}J^{\sigma};\\
&[Q_{\nu\rho},J_{\alpha}]_{FF}=a_{1}{\epsilon}_{\sigma\alpha(\nu}\partial_{\rho)}\partial^{\sigma}O+a_{2}\partial_{(\nu}T_{\rho)\alpha};\qquad\qquad\end{aligned}\begin{aligned} &a=-\frac{8}{3}\\[10.0pt]
&a_{0}=-a\\
&a_{1}=\frac{a}{2},\;a_{2}=-2ia\end{aligned}

(2.19)

2.3
Free Boson algebra from OPE

We now discuss the CFT of a single free complex boson
{\phi}
in three dimensions. The computation is set up analogously to the free fermion case, thus we now evaluate (
2.9
) using FB currents (
1.2
). We use properties of the free boson propagator:

\displaystyle F(x)={\langle}{\phi^{\dagger}}(x){\phi}(0){\rangle}={\langle}{\phi}(x){\phi^{\dagger}}(0){\rangle}=\frac{1}{4\pi}\bigg(\frac{1}{|x|}\bigg);\qquad-\partial^{2}F(x)=-\frac{1}{4\pi}\partial^{2}\bigg(\frac{1}{|x|}\bigg)=\delta(x)

(2.20)

We first analyze the action of the spin-3 charge on the fields
{\phi}
and
{\phi^{\dagger}}
:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &=\int_{V}\partial^{\mu}\bigg(J_{\mu\nu\rho}(x)\times{\phi}(0)\bigg)\\
&=\int_{V}\partial^{\mu}\bigg[\frac{1}{6}\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}F(x){\phi}(x)-\frac{1}{6}F(x)\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}{\phi}(x)+\frac{5}{2}\partial_{(\mu}F(x)\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho)}{\phi}(x)\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\par-\frac{5}{2}\partial_{(\mu}\partial_{\nu}F(x)\partial_{\rho)}{\phi}(x)-g_{(\mu\nu}\partial^{\sigma}F(x)\partial_{\rho)}\partial_{\sigma}{\phi}(x)+g_{(\mu\nu}\partial_{\rho)}\partial^{\sigma}F(x)\partial_{\sigma}{\phi}(x)\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\par-\frac{g_{(\mu\nu}}{2}\partial_{\rho)}F(x)\partial^{2}{\phi}(x)+\frac{g_{(\mu\nu}}{2}\partial^{2}F(x)\partial_{\rho)}{\phi}(x)-\frac{g_{(\mu\nu}}{10}\partial_{\rho)}\partial^{2}F(x){\phi}(x)\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad+\frac{g_{(\mu\nu}}{10}F(x)\partial_{\rho)}\partial^{2}{\phi}(x)\bigg]+\cancelto{\to 0\text{ as }r\to 0}{\text{regular}}\end{aligned}

(2.21)

After simplifying and using (
2.20
), we only end up with terms diverging as delta functions. We find:

\displaystyle[Q_{\nu\rho},{\phi}(0)]

\displaystyle=-\int_{V}\bigg(\frac{1}{6}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\delta(x){\phi}(x)-\frac{10}{6}\partial_{(\nu}{\phi}(x)\partial_{\rho)}\delta(x)+\frac{5}{6}\delta(x)\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}{\phi}(x)\bigg)=-\frac{8}{3}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}{\phi}(0)

(2.22)

We denote the coefficient multiplying the right-hand side of (
2.22
) by
b=-\frac{8}{3}
, which we will use to express other coefficients of the FB higher-spin algebra. Analogously, we find the action of the spin-3 charge on the conjugate field
{\phi^{\dagger}}
:

\displaystyle[Q_{\nu\rho},{\phi^{\dagger}}(0)]

\displaystyle=\int_{V}\partial^{\mu}\bigg(J_{\mu\nu\rho}(x)\times{\phi^{\dagger}}(0)\bigg)=\frac{8}{3}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}{\phi^{\dagger}}(0)

(2.23)

It is curious to note that, after normalizing the spin-3 current so that its two point functions are the same in FF and FB theories, we find that the action of the spin-3 charge on the fundamental fields (
2.13
) and (
2.14
) has the same coefficients as (
2.22
) and (
2.23
).
Using these relations we can now find the action of the spin-3 charge on conserved currents of the FB theory. For the scalar current, we have:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} [Q_{\nu\rho},O]_{FB}&=8[Q_{\nu\rho},{\phi^{\dagger}}]{\phi}+8{\phi^{\dagger}}[Q_{\nu\rho},{\phi}]=-\frac{64i}{3}\partial_{(\nu}\bigg(i\partial_{\rho)}{\phi^{\dagger}}{\phi}-i{\phi^{\dagger}}\partial_{\rho)}{\phi}\bigg)=-\frac{64i}{3}\partial_{(\nu}J_{\rho)}\end{aligned}

(2.24)

This is consistent with the expression found in (
2.7
). We now analyze the action of the spin-3 charge on the vector current. The charge commutes with the generator of translations
[Q_{\nu\rho},\partial]=0
, therefore:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} {}_{FB}&=i\partial_{\alpha}[Q_{\nu\rho},{\phi^{\dagger}}]{\phi}+i\partial_{\alpha}{\phi^{\dagger}}[Q_{\nu\rho},{\phi}]-i[Q_{\nu\rho},{\phi^{\dagger}}]\partial_{\alpha}{\phi}-i{\phi^{\dagger}}\partial_{\alpha}[Q_{\nu\rho},{\phi}]\\
&=-\frac{8i}{3}\bigg(\partial_{\alpha}{\phi^{\dagger}}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}{\phi}+\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}{\phi^{\dagger}}\partial_{\alpha}{\phi}\bigg)+\frac{8i}{3}\bigg(\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}{\phi^{\dagger}}{\phi}+{\phi^{\dagger}}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}{\phi}\bigg)\end{aligned}

(2.25)

The result can be rewritten in terms of single-trace primaries, defined in (
1.2
). Putting it together, the commutator
[Q_{\nu\rho},J_{\alpha}]_{FB}
can be written as:

\displaystyle[Q_{\nu\rho},J_{\alpha}]_{FB}

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{6}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\partial_{\alpha}O-\frac{i}{6}g_{\alpha(\nu}\partial_{\rho)}\partial^{2}O-\frac{16i}{3}\partial_{(\nu}T_{\rho)\alpha}

(2.26)

The expression (
2.26
) is consistent with the constraints obtained in (
2.7
), and we find that only single-trace terms appear.

For comparisons to the remainder of this text, we write the results of this Section as:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &[Q_{\nu\rho},{\phi}]_{FB}=b\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}{\phi};\\
&[Q_{\nu\rho},{\phi^{\dagger}}]_{FB}=-b\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}{\phi^{\dagger}};\\
&[Q_{\nu\rho},O]_{FB}=b_{0}\partial_{(\nu}J_{\rho)};\\
&[Q_{\nu\rho},J_{\alpha}]_{FB}=b_{1}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\partial_{\alpha}O+b_{2}g_{\alpha(\nu}\partial_{\rho)}\partial^{2}O+b_{3}\partial_{(\nu}T_{\rho)\alpha};\qquad\qquad\end{aligned}\begin{aligned} &b=-\frac{8}{3}\\[10.0pt]
&b_{0}=8ib\\
&b_{1}=-b_{2}=-\frac{ib}{16},\;b_{3}=2ib\end{aligned}

(2.27)

2.4
Higher-spin Ward identities of free and interacting theories

We make some broad remarks about the structure of higher-spin Ward identities, both in theories with exact and slightly broken higher-spin symmetry. Suppose we denote all the dynamical fields of the theory by
\phi
and that the spectrum of single-trace operators contains a tower of (slightly-)conserved higher-spin currents
J_{s}
, which are sourced by
\mathcal{A}_{s}
. The path integral of the theory reads:

\displaystyle Z[\phi,\mathcal{A}_{i}]=\int_{\phi}\mathcal{D}\phi e^{-S[\phi,\mathcal{A}_{i}]-\mathcal{A}_{0}J_{0}-\mathcal{A}_{1\mu}J_{1}^{\mu}-\mathcal{A}_{2\mu\nu}J_{2}^{\mu\nu}-...}

(2.28)

Correlators involving
J_{s}(x)
can now be built by taking derivatives
\delta/\delta\mathcal{A}_{s}(x)
and then setting
\mathcal{A}_{i}
to their background values.
In theories with slightly broken higher-spin symmetry, the exponent in (
2.28
) should be thought of as up to
\mathcal{O}(1/N)
corrections. Even so, one can build conserved charges to leading order. Furthermore, we may assume the spin-s current develops a divergence in the form of some local operator
\partial\cdot J_{s}\propto\frac{1}{N}X
, which we can formally replace by
\partial\cdot\mathcal{J}_{s}=(\partial\cdot J_{s}-\frac{1}{N}X)
and discuss Ward identities of
\mathcal{J}_{s}
in standard fashion, interpreting the resulting terms as variations of local operators.

Notably, the action
S[\phi,\mathcal{A}_{i}]
may also contain non-linear terms in the sources. As a simple example, consider a theory with a global U(1) symmetry. The action in (
2.28
) may contain a term
6
6

6

This term is a global symmetry analogue of the seagull vertex in scalar QED.
like
-\mathcal{A}_{1\mu}\mathcal{A}_{1}^{\mu}J_{0}
. Such a term would then contribute contact terms in all U(1) Ward identities containing at least two insertions of
J_{1}
and one insertion of
J_{0}
(see Appendix
C.3
).

The (almost-)conservation of a higher spin current
J_{s}^{\mu_{1}..\mu_{s}}
results from the invariance of the generating functional, to leading order in large
N
, under the corresponding global transformation, parametrized by a uniform parameter
\varepsilon
:
\delta_{\varepsilon}Z=\mathcal{O}(1/N)
. Let us now promote
\varepsilon
to a spacetime-dependent parameter
\varepsilon(x)
. Under such a transformation, the operators of the theory transform as
7
7

7

We suppress Lorentz indices on the variation as well as the infinitesimal parameter
\varepsilon
itself, which should be taken schematically.

O_{i}(x_{i})\to O_{i}(x_{i})+\delta_{\varepsilon}O_{i}(x_{i})
, so that:

\displaystyle\delta_{\varepsilon}O_{i}(x_{i})=\varepsilon(x)\delta^{(0)}O_{i}(x_{i})+\partial^{\alpha_{1}}\varepsilon(x)\delta^{(1)}_{\alpha_{1}}O_{i}(x_{i})+\partial^{\alpha_{1}}\partial^{\alpha_{2}}\varepsilon(x)\delta^{(2)}_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}O_{i}(x_{i})+...

(2.29)

Here
\delta^{(n)}O_{i}(x_{i})
stand in for local operators that define the variation. The higher-spin Ward identity is obtained by varying
\int_{\phi}\mathcal{D}\phi e^{-S}O_{1}(x_{1})O_{2}(x_{2})...O_{n}(x_{n})
. Expanding to leading order in small
\epsilon(x)
and large
N
, by using manipulations under the integral sign we find:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} 0=\int_{\phi}\mathcal{D}\phi e^{-S}\int_{x}\varepsilon(x)\bigg[&\partial\cdot\mathcal{J}_{s}(x)\prod_{i=1}^{n}O_{i}(x_{i})\\
&-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigg(\delta(x-x_{i})\delta^{(0)}O_{i}(x_{i})-\delta^{\alpha_{1}}\delta(x-x_{i})\delta^{(1)}_{\alpha_{1}}O_{i}(x_{i})+...\bigg)\prod_{i\neq j}O_{j}(x_{j})\bigg]\end{aligned}

(2.30)

Where
\mathcal{J}_{s}
is the effective spin-s current defined previously, and is exactly conserved at leading order in large
N
. We can now still require that the variation vanishes at leading order, only for
\varepsilon=const
. However, note that the contact terms on the right-hand side contain contributions where derivatives act on delta functions. Since this holds for any uniform
\varepsilon
, we arrive at the
local
higher-spin Ward identity:

\displaystyle{\langle}(\partial\cdot J_{s}(x))\prod_{i=1}^{n}O_{i}(x_{i}){\rangle}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\langle}\delta O_{i}(x_{i})\prod_{i\neq j}O_{j}(x_{j}){\rangle}+\frac{1}{N}{\langle}:X:\prod_{i=1}^{n}O_{j}(x_{j}){\rangle}

(2.31)

The
local variation
of each operator is given by
\delta O_{i}(x_{i})=\delta(x-x_{i})\delta^{(0)}O_{i}(x_{i})-\partial^{\alpha_{1}}\delta(x-x_{i})\delta^{(1)}_{\alpha_{1}}O_{i}(x_{i})+...
where the operators
\delta^{(n)}O_{i}
is defined as in (
2.29
). Inside correlation functions, this represents how a local operator
O_{i}(x_{i})
is affected by the insertion of
(\partial\cdot J_{s}(x))
. The contact terms involving derivatives acting on delta functions highlight the spacetime nature of higher-spin symmetry.

As we integrate the Ward identity (
2.31
), the variation should match the action of the spin-s charge
\int_{x}\delta O_{i}=[Q_{s},O_{i}]
. This implies that the expressions we find in Section
2
fix the local variations of operators up to a linear sum of contact terms. For example, comparing to (
2.24
), the local variation of the scalar current
O^{FB}(x_{1})
due to the insertion of
((\partial\cdot J)_{\mu\nu}(x))^{FB}
is:

\displaystyle\delta O^{FB}(x_{1})

\displaystyle=-\frac{64i}{3}\bigg(k\partial_{(\mu}\delta(x_{1}-x)J_{\nu)}(x)+(1-k)\delta(x_{1}-x)\partial_{(\mu}J_{\nu)}(x)-(\text{$\mu=\nu$ Trace terms})\bigg)

(2.32)

For some
k\in\mathbb{C}
, and analogously for other operators. As we will see in Section
3
, in cases where higher-spin symmetry is broken, it will be important to understand the higher-spin algebra and associated Ward identities in the unintegrated form (
2.31
). In the remainder of this section, we analyze Ward identities of free theories. In this case,
X=0
and one can integrate the equation over insertions of
(\partial\cdot J_{s}(x))
without creating any divergences
[
13
]
. Hence, in subsections
2.4.1
and
2.4.2
we ignore the subtleties and analyze the integrated equations directly.

2.4.1
Free-fermionic Ward Identities

Integrated higher-spin Ward identities take a simple form in the free fermion theory
[
12
,
13
]
:

\displaystyle 0=\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\langle}O_{1}(x_{1})O_{2}(x_{2})...[Q_{\mu\nu},O_{i}(x_{i})]...O_{n}(x_{n}){\rangle}_{FF}

(2.33)

In Appendix
C.1
, by using free field contractions, we verify that coefficients obtained in (
2.15
) and (
2.17
) lead to consistent relations between correlators constructed of scalar and vector currents, for general
n
-point functions. For simplicity, we focus on matching only non-contact terms, as all contact terms can be recovered by using the precise form of current operators (
1.4
) and carefully tracking the derivations. As an illustration, we analyze a simple vertex Ward identity in the main text.

\underline{\bm{{\langle}[Q_{\mu\nu},{\psi}O{\bar{\psi}}]{\rangle}_{FF}}\textbf{ Ward identity}}

Although one cannot write a gauge-invariant vertex in the full QF Chern-Simons-matter theory, it is interesting to analyze the vertex Ward identities in the free theory limit. For simplicity, we start from
8
8

8

The ratio of coefficients in
[Q_{\mu\nu},\psi]
and
[Q_{\mu\nu},{\bar{\psi}}]
is easily found from analyzing the action of
Q_{\mu\nu}
on
S_{ij}
(
2.13
), (
2.14
) and relate this to the coefficient appearing in (
2.15
). The fermion propagator from
x_{i}
to
x_{j}
is denoted by
S_{ij}
and defined similarly to (
2.10
). Note that translation invariance implies
\partial_{i}S_{ij}=-\partial_{j}S_{ij}
. We have:

\displaystyle a_{0}{\epsilon}_{\rho_{1}\rho_{2}(\mu}\partial_{2\nu)}\partial_{2}^{\rho_{1}}{\langle}{\psi}(x_{1})J^{\rho_{2}}(x_{2}){\bar{\psi}}(x_{3}){\rangle}_{FF}-\big(a\partial_{3\mu}\partial_{3\nu}-a\partial_{1\mu}\partial_{1\nu}\big){\langle}{\psi}(x_{1})O(x_{2}){\bar{\psi}}(x_{3}){\rangle}_{FF}=0

(2.34)

Using the form of the operators (
1.4
):

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} {\langle}{\psi}(x_{1})O(x_{2}){\bar{\psi}}(x_{3}){\rangle}_{FF}=&-{\langle}{\psi}(x_{1}){\bar{\psi}}{\psi}(x_{2}){\bar{\psi}}(x_{3}){\rangle}_{FF}=-S_{12}S_{23}\\
{\langle}{\psi}(x_{1})J_{\mu}(x_{2}){\bar{\psi}}(x_{3}){\rangle}_{FF}=&-{\langle}{\psi}(x_{1})i{\bar{\psi}}{\gamma}_{\mu}{\psi}(x_{2}){\bar{\psi}}(x_{3}){\rangle}_{FF}=-iS_{12}{\gamma}_{\mu}S_{23}\end{aligned}

(2.35)

The term involving the spin-1 vertex can be rewritten using the properties of (
1.3
):

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} -ia_{0}\partial_{2(\nu}\partial_{2}^{\rho_{1}}\big(S_{12}{\epsilon}_{\mu)\rho_{1}\rho_{2}}{\gamma}^{\rho_{2}}S_{23}\big)&=\par-\frac{a_{0}}{2}\partial_{2(\nu}\big(\partial_{2}^{\rho_{1}}S_{12}[{\gamma}_{\mu},{\gamma}_{\rho_{1}}]S_{23}+S_{12}[{\gamma}_{\mu},{\gamma}_{\rho_{1}}]\partial_{2}^{\rho_{1}}S_{23}\big)\\
&\to\par-\frac{a_{0}}{2}\partial_{2(\nu}\big(\partial_{1}^{\rho_{1}}S_{12}\{{\gamma}_{\mu},{\gamma}_{\rho_{1}}\}S_{23}-S_{12}\{{\gamma}_{\mu},{\gamma}_{\rho_{1}}\}\partial_{2}^{\rho_{1}}S_{23}\big)\\
&=-a_{0}\partial_{2\mu}\partial_{2\nu}S_{12}S_{23}+a_{0}S_{12}\partial_{2\mu}\partial_{2\nu}S_{23}\end{aligned}

(2.36)

The first and second line are equivalent up to EoM terms. The spin-0 vertex contributions can be rewritten as:

\displaystyle-\big(a\partial_{3\mu}\partial_{3\nu}-a\partial_{1\mu}\partial_{1\nu}\big)\big(-S_{12}S_{23}\big)

\displaystyle=aS_{12}\partial_{2\mu}\partial_{2\nu}S_{23}-a\partial_{2\mu}\partial_{2\nu}S_{12}S_{23}

(2.37)

Here we’ve used translation invariance to write all derivatives as
\partial_{2}
. Inserting (
2.36
) and (
2.37
) into (
2.34
), we find the equation is satisfied for
a_{0}=-a
, consistent with the free-fermionic OPE calculation (
2.19
). Note that free theory Ward identities are homogeneous, hence they constrain the coefficients up to an overall constant, which needs to fixed from the OPE.

2.4.2
Free-bosonic Ward Identities

Ward Identities of the spin-3 current in the free boson theory take an analogous form
[
12
,
13
]
:

\displaystyle 0=\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\langle}O_{1}(x_{1})O_{2}(x_{2})...[Q_{\mu\nu},O_{i}(x_{i})]...O_{n}(x_{n}){\rangle}_{FB}

(2.38)

The free-bosonic algebra will become important when discussing the critical limit (
{\tilde{\lambda}}\to\infty
) of QF Chern-Simons-matter theory, in the following section. For our purposes, we confine ourselves to verifying the spin-3 Ward identities of two- and three-point functions involving the scalar current
O^{FB}
and vector current
J_{\mu}^{FB}
defined in (
1.2
). Again, we will focus on matching only non-contact terms, as contact terms can be included straightforwardly by following the derivation. We write out the two-point function Ward identity, and refer to Appendix
C.2
for FB Ward identities of three-point functions.

\underline{\bm{{\langle}[Q_{\mu\nu},J_{\alpha}O]{\rangle}_{FB}}\textbf{ Ward identity}}

The only non-trivial Ward identity at the level of two-point functions comes from the action of the spin-3 charge on
{\langle}J_{\alpha}O{\rangle}_{FB}
. For simplicity, we assume only terms that appear in (
2.24
) and (
2.26
) with general coefficients, but it is easy to verify that even starting from the most general ansatz (
2.6
) leads to the same constraints. Analogously to (
2.20
), we denote the free boson propagator from
x_{i}
to
x_{j}
by
F_{ij}
. Since
{\langle}J_{s}J_{s^{\prime}}{\rangle}\propto\delta_{ss^{\prime}}
, we have:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} b_{1}\partial_{1\mu}\partial_{1\nu}\partial_{1\alpha}{\langle}O(x_{1})O(x_{2}){\rangle}_{FB}+b_{2}g_{\alpha(\mu}\partial_{1\nu)}\partial_{1}^{2}{\langle}O(x_{1})O(x_{2}){\rangle}_{FB}+b_{0}\partial_{2(\mu}{\langle}J_{\alpha}(x_{1})J_{\nu)}(x_{2}){\rangle}_{FB}=0\end{aligned}

(2.39)

We use
{\langle}O(x_{1})O(x_{2}){\rangle}_{FB}=64F_{12}^{2}
and rewrite the
{\langle}OO{\rangle}_{FB}
terms as:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\partial_{1\mu}\partial_{1\nu}\partial_{1\alpha}{\langle}O(x_{1})O(x_{2}){\rangle}_{FB}=\\[5.0pt]
&128(\partial_{1\mu}\partial_{1\nu}F_{12}\partial_{1\alpha}F_{12}+\partial_{1\mu}\partial_{1\alpha}F_{12}\partial_{1\nu}F_{12}+\partial_{1\nu}\partial_{1\alpha}F_{12}\partial_{1\mu}F_{12}+F_{12}\partial_{1\mu}\partial_{1\nu}\partial_{1\alpha}F_{12})\\[5.0pt]
&g_{\alpha(\mu}\partial_{1\nu)}\partial_{1}^{2}{\langle}O(x_{1})O(x_{2}){\rangle}_{FB}=256g_{\alpha(\mu}\partial_{1}^{\sigma}F_{12}\partial_{1\nu)}\partial_{1\sigma}F_{12}\end{aligned}

(2.40)

The spin-1 two point function is can be written as
{\langle}J_{\alpha}(x_{1})J_{\nu}(x_{2}){\rangle}_{FB}=2\partial_{1\alpha}F_{12}\partial_{1\nu}F_{12}-2F_{12}\partial_{1\alpha}\partial_{1\nu}F_{12}
. We find:

\displaystyle\partial_{2(\mu}{\langle}J_{\alpha}(x_{1})J_{\nu)}(x_{2}){\rangle}=-\partial_{1(\mu}{\langle}J_{\alpha}(x_{1})J_{\nu)}(x_{2}){\rangle}=-2\partial_{1\alpha}\partial_{1(\mu}F_{12}\partial_{1\nu)}F_{12}+2F_{12}\partial_{1\mu}\partial_{1\nu}\partial_{1\alpha}F_{12}

(2.41)

Up to contact terms
{\langle}T_{\mu\nu}(x_{1})O(x_{2}){\rangle}_{FB}=0
, which leads to an additional relation between terms:

\displaystyle 3\partial_{1\mu}F_{12}\partial_{1\nu}F_{12}-g_{\mu\nu}\partial_{1\sigma}F_{12}\partial_{1}^{\sigma}F_{12}-F_{12}\partial_{1\mu}\partial_{1\nu}F_{12}=0

(2.42)

Combining (
2.40
) - (
2.42
) with the Ward identity (
2.39
), we find:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\bigg(128b_{1}-2b_{0}+384b_{2}\bigg)\partial_{1\alpha}F_{12}\partial_{1\mu}\partial_{1\nu}F_{12}+\bigg(256b_{1}+256b_{2}\bigg)\partial_{1(\mu}F_{12}\partial_{1\nu)}\partial_{1\alpha}F_{12}\\
&+\bigg(128b_{1}+2b_{0}-128b_{2}\bigg)F_{12}\partial_{1\mu}\partial_{1\nu}\partial_{1\alpha}F_{12}=0\end{aligned}

(2.43)

Since the terms are linearly independent, this is only satisfied if
b_{2}=-b_{1}=\frac{b_{0}}{128}
, which matches the free-boson OPE results (
2.27
).

3
Critical theory Ward idenities

The FB theory has an exact higher-spin symmetry with a tower of conserved higher-spin currents. In this section, we consider a free scalar
U(N)
model, deformed by a quartic interaction
\frac{\lambda_{4}}{2N}({\phi^{\dagger}}{\phi})^{2}
. In
d=3
, this is a relevant deformation, hence by tuning the physical mass to zero and flowing to the IR we reach an interacting conformal fixed point, referred to as the critical boson (CB) theory
[
22
]
. This theory plays a further role in our analysis as the
\tilde{\lambda}\to\infty
limit of QF Chern-Simons-matter theory.

A key difference when considering higher-spin Ward identities of the CB theory relative to free theories is the fact that higher-spin symmetry is broken. In particular, the spin-3 current now develops a divergence at subleading orders in large
N
in the form of double- and triple-trace operators. The divergence
(\partial\cdot J)_{\mu\nu}
is itself a conformal primary, and is therefore constrained to the form found in
[
21
]
:

\displaystyle(\partial\cdot J)^{CB}_{\mu\nu}=\frac{A}{N}\bigg(\frac{2}{5}O\partial_{(\mu}J_{\nu)}-\frac{3}{5}J_{(\mu}\partial_{\nu)}O+\frac{g_{\mu\nu}}{5}J^{\sigma}\partial_{\sigma}O\bigg)

(3.1)

The factor
A
is related to the choice of normalization of
J_{3},J_{1},O
. The same expression was obtained in
[
14
]
, by analyzing the classical equations of motion to leading order in the ’t Hooft coupling, and then taking the critical theory limit
{\tilde{\lambda}}\to\infty
.

At the FB fixed point the scalar has conformal dimension
\Delta_{0}=1
, while at the critical fixed point its dimension is given by
\Delta_{\tilde{0}}=2+\mathcal{O}(\frac{1}{N})
. Correlators of the critical theory are related to FB correlators by a Legendre transformation with respect to the scalar current. As shown in
[
23
]
, at large
N
, momentum space relations between two- and three-point functions are remarkably simple. With the FB currents normalized as in (
1.2
), we have:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &{\langle}O(p)O(-p){\rangle}_{CB}=-{\langle}O(p)O(-p){\rangle}^{-1}_{FB}\\
&{\langle}J_{s}(p)J_{s}(-p){\rangle}_{CB}={\langle}J_{s}(p)J_{s}(-p){\rangle}_{FB}\\
&{\langle}O(p_{1})O(p_{2})O(p_{3}){\rangle}_{CB}={\langle}O(p_{1})O(p_{2})O(p_{3}){\rangle}_{FB}{\langle}O(p_{1})O(-p_{1}){\rangle}^{-1}_{FB}{\langle}O(p_{2})O(-p_{2}){\rangle}^{-1}_{FB}{\langle}O(p_{3})O(-p_{3}){\rangle}^{-1}_{FB}\\
&{\langle}J_{s_{1}}(p_{1})O(p_{2})O(p_{3}){\rangle}_{CB}={\langle}J_{s_{1}}(p_{1})O(p_{2})O(p_{3}){\rangle}_{FB}{\langle}O(p_{2})O(-p_{2}){\rangle}^{-1}_{FB}{\langle}O(p_{3})O(-p_{3}){\rangle}^{-1}_{FB}\\
&{\langle}J_{s_{1}}(p_{1})J_{s_{2}}(p_{2})O(p_{3}){\rangle}_{CB}={\langle}J_{s_{1}}(p_{1})J_{s_{2}}(p_{2})O(p_{3}){\rangle}_{FB}{\langle}O(p_{3})O(-p_{3}){\rangle}^{-1}_{FB}\\
&{\langle}J_{s_{1}}(p_{1})J_{s_{2}}(p_{2})J_{s_{3}}(p_{3}){\rangle}_{CB}={\langle}J_{s_{1}}(p_{1})J_{s_{2}}(p_{2})J_{s_{3}}(p_{3}){\rangle}_{FB}\end{aligned}

(3.2)

Unlike in previous sections, it will be necessary to analyze higher-spin Ward identities of CB theory locally. To emphasize this, we briefly analyze the three-point function
{\langle}(\partial\cdot J)_{\mu\nu}(x)J_{\alpha}(x_{1})O(x_{2}){\rangle}_{CB}
. This correlator contains non-local terms, determined by (
3.1
) and therefore if we integrate it over
x
, we may expect to pick up divergent contributions as
x\to x_{1,2}
.

On the other hand, we can first analyze the
{\langle}(\partial\cdot J)_{\mu\nu}(x)J_{\alpha}(x_{1})O(x_{2}){\rangle}_{FB}
Ward identity, and then perform the Legendre transform. In doing this, we pick up non-local terms reflecting the fact that higher-spin symmetry is broken, in addition to contact terms, which we now interpret as variations of operators in the CB theory. By ensuring that the two ways of doing the calculation lead to the same result, we will obtain constraints on the unintegrated variations (
2.29
) of
\delta O
and
\delta J_{\alpha}
in the FB and CB theories.

By viewing the CB theory as a special QF theory, we can already form an expectation for its higher-spin algebra following the arguments of Section
2.1
. The theory is invariant under parity, which further constrains the algebra to:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &[Q_{\mu\nu},O]_{CB}=\varnothing\\
&[Q_{\mu\nu},J_{\alpha}]_{CB}={\tilde{b}}_{2}\partial_{(\mu}T_{\nu)\alpha}+{\tilde{b}}_{3}\partial_{\alpha}T_{\mu\nu}+{\tilde{b}}_{4}{\epsilon}_{\alpha\rho(\mu}J_{1\nu)}J_{1}^{\rho}\end{aligned}

(3.3)

With our task being to determine the overall constants
{\tilde{b}}_{2,3,4}
as well as constraints on contact terms that integrate to (
3.3
). We find that this leads to consistent CB Ward identities for two- and three-point functions.

3.1
{\langle}(\partial\cdot J)_{\mu\nu}J_{\alpha}O{\rangle}_{CB}
calculation. CB algebra.

To capture higher-spin contact terms in
{\langle}(ip\cdot J)_{\mu\nu}(p)J_{\alpha}(p_{1})O(p_{2}){\rangle}_{FB}
we perform a 1-loop calculation using the operators defined in (
1.2
), this time including the exact form of the operators, even up to the equations of motion. To then obtain the CB result, we sum over scalar bubbles, as displayed in Figure
1
(b).

Figure 1
:
(a)
{\langle}J_{\mu\nu\rho}(p)J_{\alpha}(p_{1})O(p_{2}){\rangle}_{FB}
is obtained by summing the triangle diagram with its reflection, to account for permutations of vertices. (b) The CB correlator is obtained by summing displayed diagrams for
o_{1}=0,1,2,3,...
, also with vertex permutations.

In momentum space, we denote the insertion of a spin-
s
current of momentum
p
with
V_{s}(p,k)
, where
k
is the outgoing loop momentum.
{\langle}J_{3}J_{1}O{\rangle}_{CB}
can be computed by using vertex expressions for (
1.2
) and usual Feynman integrals:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &{\langle}J_{\mu\nu\rho}(p)J_{\alpha}(p_{1})O(p_{2}){\rangle}_{FB}=\\
&N\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\bigg(\frac{V_{\mu\nu\rho}(p,k-p_{1})V_{\alpha}(p_{1},k)V_{0}(p_{2},k+p_{2})}{(k-p_{1})^{2}k^{2}(k+p_{2})^{2}}+\frac{V_{\mu\nu\rho}(p,k-p_{2})V_{0}(p_{2},k)V_{\alpha}(p_{1},k+p_{1})}{(k-p_{2})^{2}k^{2}(k+p_{1})^{2}}\bigg)\end{aligned}

(3.4)

The resulting expression is tedious, containing a large number of terms. However, we are not necessarily interested in the full result for
{\langle}J_{\mu\nu\rho}(p)J_{\alpha}(p_{1})O(p_{2}){\rangle}_{FB}
, rather, we want to understand how the scalar and vector currents are affected by the insertion of
(ip\cdot J)_{\mu\nu}(p)
. After taking the divergence of
J_{\mu\nu\rho}
, the expression (
3.4
) reduces to a tractable number of terms. As the FB theory has exact higher-spin symmetry, we expect all terms to be contact terms, however, since the spin-3 current is not conserved in the critical theory, we expect the Legendre transform (
3.2
) to introduce non-contact terms. The computation gives:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &{\langle}(ip\cdot J)_{\mu\nu}(p)J_{\alpha}(p_{1})O(p_{2}){\rangle}_{FB}=\\
&\frac{64i}{3}\bigg(\frac{2}{5}ip_{1(\mu}-\frac{3}{5}ip_{2(\mu}\bigg){\langle}J_{\alpha}(p_{1})J_{\nu)}(-p_{1}){\rangle}_{FB}\\
&+\bigg(-\frac{1}{10}p_{1(\mu}g_{\nu)\alpha}p_{2}^{2}-\frac{1}{20}p_{1(\mu}p_{2\nu)}p_{2\alpha}+\frac{3}{40}p_{1\alpha}p_{2\mu}p_{2\nu}+\frac{1}{40}p_{1\mu}p_{1\nu}p_{2\alpha}-\frac{3}{20}p_{1\alpha}p_{1(\mu}p_{2\nu)}\\
&\qquad+\frac{1}{40}p_{1\alpha}p_{1\mu}p_{1\nu}+\frac{1}{15}p_{2(\mu}g_{\nu)\alpha}p_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{120}p_{2\mu}p_{2\nu}p_{2\alpha}\bigg){\langle}O(-p_{2})O(p_{2}){\rangle}_{FB}-\text{Trace terms}\end{aligned}

(3.5)

Where we’ve used the two-point functions in a theory of a single complex boson:

\displaystyle{\langle}O(-p_{2})O(p_{2}){\rangle}_{FB}=\frac{8}{p_{2}};\qquad\quad{\langle}J_{\alpha}(p_{1})J_{\nu}(-p_{1}){\rangle}_{FB}=\frac{p_{1\alpha}p_{1\nu}-g_{\alpha\nu}p_{1}^{2}}{16p_{1}}

(3.6)

Since all terms are polynomials of either
p_{1}
or
p_{2}
, all terms are contact terms. Trace terms are included so that the expression is exactly traceless over
\mu=\nu
, consistent with the tracelessness of
(ip\cdot J)_{\mu\nu}
. To emphasize the presence of different kinds of contact terms, we write the local Ward identity (
2.31
) in position space:

\displaystyle{\langle}(\partial\cdot J)_{\mu\nu}(x)J_{\alpha}(x_{1})O(x_{2}){\rangle}_{FB}={\langle}J_{\alpha}(x_{1})\delta O^{FB}(x_{2}){\rangle}_{FB}+{\langle}\delta J^{FB}_{\alpha}(x_{1})O(x_{2}){\rangle}_{FB}

(3.7)

With the
local variations
of the spin-0 current
\delta O^{FB}(x_{2})
and the spin-1 current
\delta J^{FB}_{\alpha}(x_{1})
in the FB theory due to the insertion of
(\partial\cdot J)_{\mu\nu}(x)
are given by:

\displaystyle\delta O^{FB}(x_{2})

\displaystyle=-\frac{64i}{3}\bigg(\frac{3}{5}\partial_{2(\mu}\delta(x_{2}-x)J_{\nu)}(x)+\frac{2}{5}\delta(x_{2}-x)\partial_{(\mu}J_{\nu)}(x)-\text{Trace}\bigg)

(3.8)

\displaystyle\delta J^{FB}_{\alpha}(x_{1})

\displaystyle=-\frac{i}{10}\partial_{1(\mu}\delta(x_{1}-x)g_{\nu)\alpha}\partial^{2}O(x)-\frac{i}{20}\partial_{1(\mu}\delta(x_{1}-x)\partial_{\nu)}\partial_{\alpha}O(x)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{3i}{40}\partial_{1\alpha}\delta(x_{1}-x)\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}O(x)\par\par-\frac{i}{40}\partial_{1\mu}\partial_{1\nu}\delta(x_{1}-x)\partial_{\alpha}O(x)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{3i}{20}\partial_{1\alpha}\partial_{1(\mu}\delta(x_{1}-x)\partial_{\nu)}O(x)+\frac{i}{40}\partial_{1\alpha}\partial_{1\mu}\partial_{1\nu}\delta(x_{1}-x)O(x)

(3.9)

\displaystyle\quad\par-\frac{i}{15}\delta(x_{1}-x)g_{\alpha(\nu}\partial_{\mu)}\partial^{2}O(x)-\frac{i}{120}\delta(x_{1}-x)\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\alpha}O(x)

\displaystyle\quad+\delta_{T}J^{FB}_{\alpha}(x_{1})+\cancel{\frac{1}{N}(OO(x)\text{ terms})}-\text{Trace}\par

We will denote the single trace terms in (
3.8
) proportional to the scalar current by
\delta_{O}J_{\alpha}^{FB}
. From the OPE we expect
\delta J_{\alpha}^{FB}
to also contain terms proportional to the stress tensor, which we denote by
\delta_{\text{T}}J^{FB}_{\alpha}
. However such terms do not show up in this calculation, since they would be proportional to the two-point function
{\langle}TO{\rangle}_{FB}=0
. On the other hand,
\delta J_{\alpha}^{FB}
may also double- and triple-trace contact terms, compatible with (
2.7
) under the condition that such terms integrate to zero. Combining this with charge conjugation, we find that only double-trace terms going like
:OO:
could in principle appear in
\delta J_{\alpha}^{FB}
, though as we will see, further analysis will render them to zero.

Even though the OPE calculation is not sensitive to the different types of contact terms, it can easily be checked that integrating the variations leads to results consistent with (
2.24
) and (
2.26
) and hence, up to contact terms, we obtain the integrated FB Ward identity (
2.39
). From consistency with the OPE, we can guess the stress-tensor terms that appear in
\delta J^{FB}_{\alpha}
up to an unfixed constant
w_{1}^{FB}\in\mathbb{C}
:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \delta_{T}J^{FB}_{\alpha}(x_{1})=&-\frac{16i}{3}\delta(x_{1}-x)\partial_{(\mu}T_{\nu)\alpha}(x)\par+w_{1}^{FB}\bigg(\delta(x_{1}-x)\partial_{\alpha}T_{\mu\nu}(x)+\partial_{\alpha}\delta(x_{1}-x)T_{\mu\nu}(x)\bigg)\end{aligned}

(3.10)

Note that terms involving
T_{\mu\nu}
may still appear within the local variation
\delta J_{\alpha}^{FB}
. Equation (
2.26
) only restricts such terms from appearing after integration, however we can write a linear sum of contact terms that vanishes after integrating Ward identities.

Including the correct contact terms in free theories is essential for the higher-spin Ward identities to be satisfied locally. Generally, the Legendre transformation can generate non-contact terms. These non-contact terms should match the double-trace contributions developed by
(\partial\cdot J)_{\mu\nu}^{CB}
as we break higher-spin symmetry. The local CB Ward identity
{\langle}[Q_{\mu\nu},J_{\alpha}O]{\rangle}_{CB}
is obtained simply by Legendre transforming the FB result:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} {\langle}(ip\cdot J)_{\mu\nu}(p)J_{\alpha}(p_{1})O(p_{2}){\rangle}_{CB}&={\langle}(ip\cdot J)_{\mu\nu}(p)J_{\alpha}(p_{1})O(p_{2}){\rangle}_{FB}{\langle}O(p_{2})O(-p_{2}){\rangle}^{-1}_{FB}\\
&={\langle}\delta J^{FB}_{\alpha}(p_{1})O(p_{2}){\rangle}_{FB}{\langle}O(p_{2})O(-p_{2}){\rangle}^{-1}_{FB}\\
&\quad+{\langle}J_{\alpha}(p_{1})\delta O^{FB}(p_{2}){\rangle}_{FB}{\langle}O(p_{2})O(-p_{2}){\rangle}^{-1}_{FB}\end{aligned}

(3.11)

The two terms on the right-hand side can be evaluated using (
3.6
) and the variations (
3.8
), (
3.9
). We then find that the second term contributes only as a sum of contact terms in
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
. By choosing a specific regularization scheme, these terms can all be removed from
{\langle}(ip\cdot J)_{\mu\nu}(p)J_{\alpha}(p_{1})O(p_{2}){\rangle}_{CB}
. As discussed in Section
2.4
, the appearance of contact terms in Ward identities can be traced back to non-linear source terms in the generating functional of the theory. Thus, we set
{\langle}J_{\alpha}(p_{1})\delta O^{FB}(p_{2}){\rangle}_{FB}{\langle}O(p_{2})O(-p_{2}){\rangle}^{-1}_{FB}=0
, which assumes adding appropriate counterterms to the CB theory.

Let’s examine how the equation behaves when integrated in position space over the insertion of
(\partial\cdot J)_{\mu\nu}(x)
, which is equivalent to setting
p=0
in (
3.11
). The left-hand side can now be separated into two contributions, the first coming from the divergence (
3.1
) of
J_{3}
and the second, coming from the CB algebra.
As
(ip\cdot J)_{\mu\nu}(p)
is no longer a zero-operator, we generically expect to find divergences as
p_{1,2}
approach
p
. This is precisely compensated by the first term on the right-hand side of (
3.11
):

\displaystyle{\langle}(ip\cdot J)_{\mu\nu}(p)J_{\alpha}(p_{1})O(p_{2}){\rangle}_{CB}\vline_{p=0\atop\text{divergent as }p_{2}=-p_{1}\to p}={\langle}\delta J^{FB}_{\alpha}(p_{1})O(p_{2}){\rangle}_{FB}{\langle}O(p_{2})O(-p_{2}){\rangle}^{-1}_{FB}

(3.12)

From here we find the normalization constant
A=\frac{64i}{3}
in (
3.1
).

The remaining terms are finite as
p_{1,2}\to p
and by (
2.2
) correspond precisely to the CB higher-spin algebra:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &{\langle}[Q_{\mu\nu},J_{\alpha}(p_{1})]O(p_{2}){\rangle}_{CB}+{\langle}J_{\alpha}(p_{1})[Q_{\mu\nu},O(p_{2})]{\rangle}_{CB}={\langle}(ip\cdot J)_{\mu\nu}(p)J_{\alpha}(p_{1})O(p_{2}){\rangle}_{CB}\vline_{p=0\atop\text{finite as }p_{2}=-p_{1}\to p}\\
&={\langle}J_{\alpha}(p_{1})\delta O^{FB}(p_{2}){\rangle}_{FB}{\langle}O(p_{2})O(-p_{2}){\rangle}^{-1}_{FB}=0\end{aligned}

(3.13)

Which is consistent with our expectation (
3.3
). We can write the
local variations
of the spin-0 current
\delta O^{CB}(x_{2})
and the spin-1 current
\delta J^{CB}_{\alpha}(x_{1})
in the CB theory, under the insertion of
(\partial\cdot J)_{\mu\nu}(x)
, as:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \delta O^{CB}(x_{2})&=\varnothing\\
\delta J^{CB}_{\alpha}(x_{1})&=\delta_{T}J^{CB}_{\alpha}(x_{1})+{\tilde{b}}_{4}\delta(x_{1}-x){\epsilon}_{\alpha\rho(\mu}:J_{\nu)}J^{\rho}:(x)\end{aligned}

(3.14)

\delta J^{CB}_{\alpha}
can have terms proportional to the stress-tensor which we write as
\delta_{T}J^{CB}_{\alpha}
. Furthermore, as the scalar current now has dimension
\Delta_{\tilde{0}}=2+\mathcal{O}(1/N)
, the only other contribution allowed by conformal invariance is the double-trace term
:J_{1}J_{1}:
. Since the corresponding term in (
3.3
) does not have any derivatives, within the unintegrated variation (
3.14
) it shows up as a unique contact term. To constrain the algebra further, we consider Ward identities of three-point functions.

3.2
CB resummation. Bowtie diagrams.

There are four possible three-point function Ward identities that we can analyze, involving the spin-3 (pseudo) charge
Q_{\mu\nu}
acting on a combination of scalar and vector currents. By interpreting these as diagrammatic summation statements, we obtain further constraints on the CB algebra. Two of the four Ward identities simplify considerably, as some of the appearing correlators vanish.

\underline{\bm{{\langle}[Q_{\mu\nu},OOO]{\rangle}_{CB}}\textbf{ Ward identity}}

Since the scalar current remains unaffected by by the spin-3 current, we may only check that the Ward identity is satisfied, however it does not depend on the higher-spin algebra. Using (
2.31
), we write out the equation in position space:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &{\langle}(\partial\cdot J)_{\nu\rho}(x)O(x_{1})O(x_{2})O(x_{3}){\rangle}_{CB}=\\
&{\langle}\delta O^{CB}(x_{1})O(x_{2})O(x_{3}){\rangle}_{CB}+\par\frac{64i}{3}\bigg(\frac{2}{5}{\langle}O(x)O(x_{1}){\rangle}_{CB}\partial_{(\mu}{\langle}J_{\nu)}(x)O(x_{2})O(x_{3}){\rangle}_{CB}\\
&-\frac{3}{5}\partial_{(\mu}{\langle}O(x)O(x_{1}){\rangle}_{CB}{\langle}J_{\nu)}(x)O(x_{2})O(x_{3}){\rangle}_{CB}-\text{ Trace}\bigg)+\begin{Bmatrix}1\leftrightarrow 2\end{Bmatrix}+\begin{Bmatrix}1\leftrightarrow 3\end{Bmatrix}\end{aligned}

(3.15)

The terms proportional to
\delta O^{CB}
are zero. The terms inside the bracket are the leading order in large
N
contributions from the local divergence of
(\partial\cdot J_{3})^{CB}
. Since
{\langle}J_{1}OO{\rangle}_{CB}
and
{\langle}J_{1}OOO{\rangle}_{CB}
vanish by charge conjugation, we conclude that the Ward identity trivially consistent.

\underline{\bm{{\langle}[Q_{\mu\nu},J_{\alpha}OO]{\rangle}_{CB}}\textbf{ Ward identity}}

We next write the local Ward identity coming from the insertion of
(\partial\cdot J)_{\mu\nu}^{CB}
into
{\langle}J_{\alpha}OO{\rangle}_{CB}
. Combining (
2.31
) with (
3.1
) and (
3.14
), we find:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &{\langle}(ip\cdot J)_{\mu\nu}(p)J_{\alpha}(p_{1})O(p_{2})O(p_{3}){\rangle}_{CB}={\langle}\delta_{T}J^{CB}_{\alpha}(p_{1})O(p_{2})O(p_{3}){\rangle}_{CB}+\\
&\frac{64i}{3}\bigg[\bigg(ip_{1(\mu}+\frac{3}{5}ip_{(\mu}-\text{Trace}\bigg){\langle}J_{\nu)}(-p_{1})J_{\alpha}(p_{1}){\rangle}_{CB}{\langle}O(p+p_{1})O(p_{2})O(p_{3}){\rangle}_{CB}\\
&\qquad\par+\bigg(ip_{2(\mu}+\frac{3}{5}ip_{(\mu}-\text{Trace}\bigg){\langle}O(p_{2})O(-p_{2}){\rangle}_{CB}{\langle}J_{\alpha}(p_{1})J_{\nu)}(p+p_{2})O(p_{3}){\rangle}_{CB}+\begin{Bmatrix}2\leftrightarrow 3\end{Bmatrix}\bigg]\end{aligned}

(3.16)

The first term on the right-hand side comes from the higher-spin algebra of the CB theory (
3.14
), while the remaining terms come from the local divergence (
3.1
) developed by the spin-3 current. Our goal is to compare this expression to the Legendre-transformed free theory answer. This is a sum of corrected free theory diagrams and bowtie diagrams
9
9

9

The minus sign in the second line of (
3.17
) appears due to resummation of a geometric series.
(Figure
2
):

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &{\langle}(ip\cdot J)_{\mu\nu}(p)J_{\alpha}(p_{1})O(p_{2})O(p_{3}){\rangle}_{CB}=\\
&{\langle}O(p_{2})O(-p_{2}){\rangle}_{FB}^{-1}{\langle}O(p_{3})O(-p_{3}){\rangle}_{FB}^{-1}{\langle}(ip\cdot J)_{\mu\nu}(p)J_{\alpha}(p_{1})O(p_{2})O(p_{3}){\rangle}_{FB}\\
&-\bigg[\frac{{\langle}(ip\cdot J)_{\mu\nu}(p)J_{\alpha}(p_{1})O(-p-p_{1}){\rangle}_{FB}{\langle}O(p+p_{1})O(p_{2})O(p_{3}){\rangle}_{FB}}{{\langle}O(-p-p_{1})O(p+p_{1}){\rangle}_{FB}{\langle}O(p_{2})O(-p_{2}){\rangle}_{FB}{\langle}O(p_{3})O(-p_{3}){\rangle}_{FB}}\\
&\qquad\par+\bigg(\frac{{\langle}(ip\cdot J)_{\mu\nu}(p)O(p_{2})O(-p-p_{2}){\rangle}_{FB}{\langle}J_{\alpha}(p_{1})O(p+p_{2})O(p_{3}){\rangle}_{FB}}{{\langle}O(-p-p_{2})O(p+p_{2}){\rangle}_{FB}{\langle}O(p_{3})O(-p_{3}){\rangle}_{FB}}+\begin{Bmatrix}2\leftrightarrow 3\end{Bmatrix}\bigg)\bigg]\end{aligned}

(3.17)

To derive constraints on the algebra, it will prove sufficient to compare terms in (
3.16
) and (
3.17
) and that are not contact terms
10
10

10

After integrating over
x
in position space, i.e. setting
p=0
in momentum space, such terms are those that remain non-contact terms in the integrated Ward identity.
in at least two of the three momenta
p_{1,2,3}
, while the difference in any other contact terms between the two equations can be included by adding appropriate counterterms to the generating functional of the theory. We will postpone a more careful discussion of higher-spin contact terms to Section
4
, of which the CB theory contact terms are a special case.

Figure 2
:
Diagrams contributing to
{\langle}(ip\cdot J)_{\mu\nu}(p)J_{\alpha}(p_{1})O(p_{2})O(p_{3}){\rangle}
, up to vertex permutations: The FB correlator is the sum of diagrams (a) with
o_{1,2}=0
. The CB correlator is obtained by summing all diagrams for all nonnegative combinations of
o_{i}
.

In (
3.17
), let us first consider the terms appearing in the square brackets. The first term is proportional to the scalar three-point function
{\langle}OOO{\rangle}_{CB}
. As observed in
[
23
]
, this correlator is at most a constant in momentum space. Hence the entire term is only a function of
p
and
p_{1}
which we neglect in this analysis. The remaining bowtie diagram terms are proportional to
{\langle}J_{1}OO{\rangle}_{CB}
which vanishes by charge conjugation. The only non-contact terms on the right-hand side of (
3.17
) come from expanding
{\langle}(ip\cdot J)_{\mu\nu}(p)J_{\alpha}(p_{1})O(p_{2})O(p_{3}){\rangle}_{FB}
using the FB Ward identity and (
3.2
):

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &{\langle}(ip\cdot J)_{\mu\nu}(p)J_{\alpha}(p_{1})O(p_{2})O(p_{3}){\rangle}_{CB}={\langle}O(p_{1})O(-p_{1}){\rangle}_{CB}{\langle}\delta_{O}J^{FB}_{\alpha}(p_{1})O(p_{2})O(p_{3}){\rangle}_{CB}\\
&+{\langle}\delta_{T}J^{FB}_{\alpha}(p_{1})O(p_{2})O(p_{3}){\rangle}_{CB}+\bigg({\langle}O(p_{2})O(-p_{2}){\rangle}_{CB}{\langle}J_{\alpha}(p_{1})\delta O^{FB}(p_{2})O(p_{3}){\rangle}_{CB}+\begin{Bmatrix}2\leftrightarrow 3\end{Bmatrix}\bigg)\end{aligned}

(3.18)

Notice that we’ve left out the terms coming from the double-trace contributions of the FB algebra
\delta J^{FB}\propto\frac{1}{N}:OO:
on the right-hand side. Schematically, these terms would contribute as non-contact terms of the form
{\langle}OO{\rangle}_{CB}{\langle}OO{\rangle}_{CB}
at leading order in
N
. However, it’s easy to verify that one cannot kinematically match such terms to any contributions appearing on the right-hand side of the Ward identity (
3.16
). Hence we find that the FB algebra (
3.9
) does not contain any double-trace terms.

The term on the right-hand side containing
\delta_{O}J^{FB}_{\alpha}
is proportional to the three-point function
{\langle}OOO{\rangle}_{CB}
. As discussed, this correlator is a constant in momentum space and the corresponding term is only a function of
p_{1}
, which we can neglect. By using the FB algebra (
3.8
), the term in brackets can actually be rewritten as coming from the divergence of
(\partial\cdot J_{3})^{CB}
, given by (
3.1
):

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &{\langle}O(p_{2})O(-p_{2}){\rangle}_{CB}{\langle}J_{\alpha}(p_{1})\delta O^{FB}(p_{2})O(p_{3}){\rangle}_{CB}=\\
&\frac{64i}{3}\bigg(\frac{3}{5}ip_{(\mu}+ip_{2(\mu}-\text{Trace}\bigg){\langle}O(p_{2})O(-p_{2}){\rangle}_{CB}{\langle}J_{\alpha}(p_{1})J_{\nu)}(p+p_{2})O(p_{3}){\rangle}_{CB}\end{aligned}

(3.19)

We now combine (
3.18
) and (
3.19
) and compare to (
3.16
). In the Ward identity (
3.16
), the term on the right hand side proportional to
{\langle}OOO{\rangle}_{CB}
may be ignored in this analysis, as it is a contact term in
p_{2,3}
. Matching the non-contact terms in
p_{1,2,3}
between the two approaches, we end up with:

\displaystyle{\langle}\delta_{T}J^{CB}_{\alpha}(p_{1})O(p_{2})O(p_{3}){\rangle}_{CB}={\langle}\delta_{T}J^{FB}_{\alpha}(p_{1})O(p_{2})O(p_{3}){\rangle}_{CB}

(3.20)

Which is only consistent if
\delta_{T}J_{\alpha}^{CB}=\delta_{T}J_{\alpha}^{FB}
. We thus find that terms in the local variation of the spin-1 current that are not related to the scalar current are identical between the free and critical theories. Presumably, this is not surprising, as the transformation between the two theories only affects the scalar
O
, at least at the level of single-trace terms.

\underline{\bm{{\langle}[Q_{\mu\nu},J_{\alpha}J_{\beta}O]{\rangle}_{CB}}\textbf{ Ward identity}}

Writing out (
2.31
), we find contributions both from the CB algebra (
3.14
), and the divergence of the spin-3 current:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &{\langle}(\partial\cdot J)_{\mu\nu}(x)J_{\alpha}(x_{1})J_{\alpha}(x_{2})O(x_{3}){\rangle}_{CB}={\langle}J_{\alpha}(x_{1})J_{\beta}(x_{2})\delta O(x_{3}){\rangle}_{CB}\\
&+\bigg({\langle}\delta_{T}J_{\alpha}^{CB}(x_{1})J_{\beta}(x_{2})O(x_{3}){\rangle}_{CB}+\frac{{\tilde{b}}_{4}}{N}\delta(x_{1}-x){\epsilon}_{\alpha\sigma(\mu}{\langle}J_{\nu)}(x)J^{\sigma}(x)J_{\beta}(x_{2})O(x_{3}){\rangle}_{CB}+\begin{Bmatrix}1\leftrightarrow 2\\
\alpha\leftrightarrow\beta\end{Bmatrix}\bigg)\\
&+\frac{64i}{3}\bigg[\bigg(\frac{2}{5}{\langle}O(x)O(x_{1}){\rangle}_{CB}\partial_{(\mu}{\langle}J_{\nu)}(x)O(x_{2})O(x_{3}){\rangle}_{CB}-\frac{3}{5}\partial_{(\mu}{\langle}O(x)O(x_{1}){\rangle}_{CB}{\langle}J_{\nu)}(x)O(x_{2})O(x_{3}){\rangle}_{CB}\\
&\qquad\qquad\par+\begin{Bmatrix}1\leftrightarrow 2\\
\alpha\leftrightarrow\beta\end{Bmatrix}\bigg)+\bigg(\frac{2}{5}{\langle}O(x)O(x_{1}){\rangle}_{CB}\partial_{(\mu}{\langle}J_{\nu)}(x)O(x_{2})O(x_{3}){\rangle}_{CB}\\
&\qquad\qquad-\frac{3}{5}\partial_{(\mu}{\langle}O(x)O(x_{1}){\rangle}_{CB}{\langle}J_{\nu)}(x)O(x_{2})O(x_{3}){\rangle}_{CB}\bigg)-\text{Trace terms}\bigg]\end{aligned}

(3.21)

The three-point functions appearing on the right-hand side go as
{\langle}JOO{\rangle}_{CB}
,
{\langle}TJO{\rangle}_{CB}
,
{\langle}JJJ{\rangle}_{CB}
. These correlators all vanish, as was argued in
[
24
]
. The remaining term comes with a
{\tilde{b}}_{4}/N
factor, and therefore at leading order only contributes as
{\tilde{b}}_{4}{\langle}JJ{\rangle}_{CB}{\langle}JO{\rangle}_{CB}
. However as
{\langle}J_{s}J_{s^{\prime}}{\rangle}_{CB}\propto\delta_{ss^{\prime}}
, this contribution also vanishes, and we find that the right-hand side of the Ward identity is zero. This is consistent, as the left-hand side vanishes by charge conjugation, and the Ward identity is trivial.

\underline{\bm{{\langle}[Q_{\mu\nu},J_{\alpha}J_{\beta}J_{\gamma}]{\rangle}_{CB}}\textbf{ Ward identity}}

The final CB Ward identity constrains the double-trace terms appearing in (
3.14
). We can write out the Ward identity (
2.31
) using the algebra and the divergence (
3.1
). We also use
A=\frac{64i}{3}
, obtained from the
{\langle}[Q_{\mu\nu},J_{\alpha}O]{\rangle}_{CB}
Ward identity, to find:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &{\langle}(ip\cdot J)_{\mu\nu}(p)J_{\alpha}(p_{1})J_{\beta}(p_{2})J_{\gamma}(p_{3}){\rangle}_{CB}=\\
&\frac{64i}{3}\bigg(ip_{1(\mu}+\frac{3}{5}ip_{(\mu}-\text{Trace}\bigg){\langle}J_{\alpha}(p_{1})J_{\nu)}(-p_{1}){\rangle}_{CB}{\langle}O(p+p_{1})J_{\beta}(p_{2})J_{\gamma}(p_{3}){\rangle}_{CB}\\
&+{\langle}\delta_{T}J^{CB}_{\alpha}(p_{1})J_{\beta}(p_{2})J_{\gamma}(p_{3}){\rangle}_{CB}+{\tilde{b}}_{4}{\epsilon}_{\alpha\sigma(\mu}{\langle}J_{\nu)}(p+p_{1})J_{\beta}(p_{2}){\rangle}_{CB}{\langle}J^{\sigma}(-p-p_{1})J_{\gamma}(p_{3}){\rangle}_{CB}\\
&+\begin{Bmatrix}1\leftrightarrow 2\\
\alpha\leftrightarrow\beta\end{Bmatrix}+\begin{Bmatrix}1\leftrightarrow 3\\
\alpha\leftrightarrow\gamma\end{Bmatrix}\end{aligned}

(3.22)

On the right-hand side, the first term results from the non-zero divergence of the spin-3 current in the CB theory. The remaining terms come from the CB algebra. In particular, note that the double-trace terms of (
3.14
) here appear as a disconnected contribution
{\langle}JJ{\rangle}_{CB}{\langle}JJ{\rangle}_{CB}
at leading order in large
N
.

Figure 3
:
{\langle}(ip\cdot J)_{\mu\nu}(p)J_{\alpha}(p_{1})J_{\beta}(p_{2})J_{\gamma}(p_{3}){\rangle}
diagrams, up to vertex permutations: The FB theory diagrams are given by (a), and after Legendre transforming, we add bowtie diagrams (b) for all
o_{1}\geq 0
. There are no vertex corrections.

We can now try to obtain the same correlator Legendre transforming the FB result. Since
